    \makeatletter
    \newsavebox\pandoc@box
    \newcommand*\pandocbounded[1]{%
      \sbox\pandoc@box{#1}%
      % scaling factors for width and height
      \Gscale@div\@tempa\textheight{\dimexpr\ht\pandoc@box+\dp\pandoc@box\relax}%
      \Gscale@div\@tempb\linewidth{\wd\pandoc@box}%
      % select the smaller of both
      \ifdim\@tempb\p@<\@tempa\p@
        \let\@tempa\@tempb
      \fi
      % scaling accordingly (\@tempa < 1)
      \ifdim\@tempa\p@<\p@
        \scalebox{\@tempa}{\usebox\pandoc@box}%
      % scaling not needed, use as it is
      \else
        \usebox{\pandoc@box}%
      \fi
    }
    \makeatother

\def\PY@reset{\let\PY@it=\relax \let\PY@bf=\relax%
    \let\PY@ul=\relax \let\PY@tc=\relax%
    \let\PY@bc=\relax \let\PY@ff=\relax}
\def\PY@tok#1{\csname PY@tok@#1\endcsname}
\def\PY@toks#1+{\ifx\relax#1\empty\else%
    \PY@tok{#1}\expandafter\PY@toks\fi}
\def\PY@do#1{\PY@bc{\PY@tc{\PY@ul{%
    \PY@it{\PY@bf{\PY@ff{#1}}}}}}}
\def\PY#1#2{\PY@reset\PY@toks#1+\relax+\PY@do{#2}}

    % For linebreaks inside Verbatim environment from package fancyvrb.
    \makeatletter
        \newbox\Wrappedcontinuationbox
        \newbox\Wrappedvisiblespacebox
        \newcommand*\Wrappedvisiblespace {\textcolor{red}{\textvisiblespace}}
        \newcommand*\Wrappedcontinuationsymbol {\textcolor{red}{\llap{\tiny$\m@th\hookrightarrow$}}}
        \newcommand*\Wrappedcontinuationindent {3ex }
        \newcommand*\Wrappedafterbreak {\kern\Wrappedcontinuationindent\copy\Wrappedcontinuationbox}
        % Take advantage of the already applied Pygments mark-up to insert
        % potential linebreaks for TeX processing.
        %        {, <, #, %, $, ' and ": go to next line.
        %        _, }, ^, &, >, - and ~: stay at end of broken line.
        % Use of \textquotesingle for straight quote.
        \newcommand*\Wrappedbreaksatspecials {%
            \def\PYGZus{\discretionary{\char`\_}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\_}}%
            \def\PYGZob{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\char`\{}{\char`\{}}%
            \def\PYGZcb{\discretionary{\char`\}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\}}}%
            \def\PYGZca{\discretionary{\char`\^}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\^}}%
            \def\PYGZam{\discretionary{\char`\&}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\&}}%
            \def\PYGZlt{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\char`\<}{\char`\<}}%
            \def\PYGZgt{\discretionary{\char`\>}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\>}}%
            \def\PYGZsh{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\char`\#}{\char`\#}}%
            \def\PYGZpc{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\char`\%}{\char`\%}}%
            \def\PYGZdl{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\char`\$}{\char`\$}}%
            \def\PYGZhy{\discretionary{\char`\-}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\-}}%
            \def\PYGZsq{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\textquotesingle}{\textquotesingle}}%
            \def\PYGZdq{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\char`\"}{\char`\"}}%
            \def\PYGZti{\discretionary{\char`\~}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\~}}%
        }
        % Some characters . , ; ? ! / are not pygmentized.
        % This macro makes them "active" and they will insert potential linebreaks
        \newcommand*\Wrappedbreaksatpunct {%
            \lccode`\~`\.\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\.}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\.}}}%
            \lccode`\~`\,\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\,}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\,}}}%
            \lccode`\~`\;\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\;}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\;}}}%
            \lccode`\~`\:\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\:}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\:}}}%
            \lccode`\~`\?\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\?}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\?}}}%
            \lccode`\~`\!\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\!}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\!}}}%
            \lccode`\~`\/\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\/}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\/}}}%
            \catcode`\.\active
            \catcode`\,\active
            \catcode`\;\active
            \catcode`\:\active
            \catcode`\?\active
            \catcode`\!\active
            \catcode`\/\active
            \lccode`\~`\~
        }
    \makeatother

    \let\OriginalVerbatim=\Verbatim
    \makeatletter
    \renewcommand{\Verbatim}[1][1]{%
        %\parskip\z@skip
        \sbox\Wrappedcontinuationbox {\Wrappedcontinuationsymbol}%
        \sbox\Wrappedvisiblespacebox {\FV@SetupFont\Wrappedvisiblespace}%
        \def\FancyVerbFormatLine ##1{\hsize\linewidth
            \vtop{\raggedright\hyphenpenalty\z@\exhyphenpenalty\z@
                \doublehyphendemerits\z@\finalhyphendemerits\z@
                \strut ##1\strut}%
        }%
        % If the linebreak is at a space, the latter will be displayed as visible
        % space at end of first line, and a continuation symbol starts next line.
        % Stretch/shrink are however usually zero for typewriter font.
        \def\FV@Space {%
            \nobreak\hskip\z@ plus\fontdimen3\font minus\fontdimen4\font
            \discretionary{\copy\Wrappedvisiblespacebox}{\Wrappedafterbreak}
            {\kern\fontdimen2\font}%
        }%

        % Allow breaks at special characters using \PYG... macros.
        \Wrappedbreaksatspecials
        % Breaks at punctuation characters . , ; ? ! and / need catcode=\active
        \OriginalVerbatim[#1,codes*=\Wrappedbreaksatpunct]%
    }
    \makeatother

\begin{document}
    
    \maketitle
    
    

    
    \section{Pipeline 2: Are Machines Able to
Love?}\label{pipeline-2-are-machines-able-to-love}

\section{0. Executive Summary}\label{executive-summary}

In my last assignment, I went old-school (well, kind of) and used TF-IDF
with Naive Bayes and SVM to figure out whether a message came from me or
my boyfriend. That experiment used a smaller subset --- ``just'' 20k
messages --- and yielded a test accuracy of around 66\%. This time, I
scaled things up and reran the same models on the full dataset
(approximately 110k messages). The results? Pretty much the same ---
steady at 66\% accuracy, confirming what I'd already seen.

In this assignment, I also extended the pipeline starting from Section
8. That's where the real glow-up begins --- I bring in BERT, a big-shot
transformer model, for some transfer learning magic. I used it in two
ways --- first as an end-to-end classifier (spoiler: didn't improve
much), and then as a powerful word-embedding engine. That second
strategy paid off. By combining BERT embeddings with an SVM classifier,
I pushed the test accuracy all the way up to \textbf{95\%}, building a
high-performing model that's both expressive and reliable. To go beyond,
I also implemented an SVM from scratch, which achieved similar
performance to the scikit-learn version.

Then, as I was feeling adventurous, I tried building a text generator
using an LSTM --- because why not teach a neural network to reply my
boyfriend for me? To take my explanations to the next level, in Section
9.2, I made a video in Section 9.2 explaining how LSTMs work.

So, let's find out once and for all: \textbf{Are machines able to love?}

Or at least\ldots{} can they text like they do?

\textbf{If you have already read Assignment 1, you can skip to Section 8
to jump into the fun new stuff.}

\section{1. Introduction}\label{introduction}

For the past 3 years and 5 months, my boyfriend Arthur and I have
chatted on WhatsApp almost every single day. From cute good mornings to
random deep conversations, our messages tell the story of how we got to
know each other and how our relationship evolved. But what if a machine
could learn to tell us apart just from the way we text?

This project is the start of an exploration of our communication styles
using machine learning. I have a feeling my boyfriend is way more dry
and direct than I am (while I probably overuse emojis, cute words,
repeated letters, and exclamation marks). By training a model on our
conversations, I want to see if these patterns hold up --- and
hopefully, this experiment won't lead to a relationship discussion!

In this second draft assignment, I will focus on further improving my
classification by bringing a model that understands context. While
achieving a reasonable results, there is still room for improvement.
Additionally, I will go beyond and start generating text.

    \subsection{2. Data}\label{data}

\subsubsection{2.1 Data Description}\label{data-description}

I will use my Whatsapp chat with my boyfriend as data. It is important
to note that he gave me permission to use this data in this assignment.
I used regular expressions (regex) to extract structured data from the
WhatsApp chat log and convert it into a pandas DataFrame for analysis.
WhatsApp messages follow a specific format, including a timestamp,
sender's name, and message content. Using regex, I identified this
pattern and extracted only the relevant parts---ignoring system messages
or formatting inconsistencies. I created a data frame with the sender
and the message, and this is the data that I will use to train my model.

    \subsubsection{2.2 Load the Data \& Data
Cleaning}\label{load-the-data-data-cleaning}

    I will perform the process I described above in the code cell below.

    \subsection{2.3 Data Pre-processing}\label{data-pre-processing}

For the sake of simplicity, I will label the senders as 1 (myself) and 0
(Arthur).

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{6}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Map labels (1 for Mariana, 0 for Arthur)}
\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Sender}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Sender}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{map}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{\PYZob{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Mariana Jannotti}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{1}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Arthur}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{0}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    Before jumping into text preprocessing, I'll first split the data into
training and test sets to prevent any data leakage. Instead of using the
entire dataset, I'll randomly sample a smaller portion for both training
and testing. Using the full dataset would create insanely large
multidimensional vectors that would be difficult to handle. While I
could explore ways to manage this in future assignments, for now, I want
to keep things simple and work with a more manageable dataset. I will
sample the same amount of text messages from each sender to try to have
a balanced dataset, and the ratio of training data to testing data will
be 90-10.

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{7}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Step 1: Balance the dataset by sampling 69,568 messages from Sender \PYZdq{}0\PYZdq{}}
\PY{n}{balanced\PYZus{}0} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Sender}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{==} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{0}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{sample}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{n}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{69568}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{random\PYZus{}state}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{42}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{balanced\PYZus{}1} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Sender}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{==} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{1}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}  \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Already has 69,568 messages}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Combine the balanced data}
\PY{n}{balanced\PYZus{}df} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{pd}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{concat}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{balanced\PYZus{}0}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{balanced\PYZus{}1}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} save to csv}
\PY{n}{balanced\PYZus{}df}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{to\PYZus{}csv}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{balanced\PYZus{}dataset.csv}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{index}\PY{o}{=}\PY{k+kc}{False}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Step 2: Perform a 9:1 train\PYZhy{}test split}
\PY{n}{train\PYZus{}0} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{balanced\PYZus{}0}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{sample}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{frac}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mf}{0.9}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{random\PYZus{}state}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{42}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{train\PYZus{}1} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{balanced\PYZus{}1}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{sample}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{frac}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mf}{0.9}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{random\PYZus{}state}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{42}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n}{test\PYZus{}0} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{balanced\PYZus{}0}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{drop}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{train\PYZus{}0}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{index}\PY{p}{)}  \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Remaining 10\PYZpc{}}
\PY{n}{test\PYZus{}1} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{balanced\PYZus{}1}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{drop}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{train\PYZus{}1}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{index}\PY{p}{)}  \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Remaining 10\PYZpc{}}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Step 3: Combine and shuffle the train and test datasets}
\PY{n}{train\PYZus{}df} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{pd}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{concat}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{train\PYZus{}0}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{train\PYZus{}1}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{sample}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{frac}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{random\PYZus{}state}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{42}\PY{p}{)}  \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Shuffle}
\PY{n}{test\PYZus{}df} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{pd}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{concat}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{test\PYZus{}0}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{test\PYZus{}1}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{sample}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{frac}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{random\PYZus{}state}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{42}\PY{p}{)}  \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Shuffle}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    Now that I have different test and train sets and I can guarantee that
they will never see each other, we can talk about text preprocessing.
When working with text data, we need to preprocess it so that a machine
learning model can actually make sense of it. The tricky part is that
text messages can vary in length and contain a diverse range of words.
Essentially, the total set of words in our dataset represents the
combined vocabulary that Arthur and I use, which is quite large! Words
are categorical variables, but a naive approach like one-hot encoding
wouldn't work well because the resulting vectors would be massive and
sparse, making it an inefficient representation.

A common workaround is the \textbf{Bag of Words (BoW) model}, which
tokenizes text into individual words and ignores their order. If we
define \(D\) as the total number of unique words (or tokens) in our
dataset, we could represent each message as a \(D\)-dimensional vector
where each element corresponds to the number of times a specific word
appears in that message.

However, this approach comes with a problem: certain words appear
frequently in many messages, even though they don't carry much
meaningful information. If we naively count occurrences, these words
could dominate the representation of a message. That's where
\textbf{Term Frequency-Inverse Document Frequency (TF-IDF)} comes in.

TF-IDF helps balance word importance by scaling word frequency using
logarithmic weighting. The key idea is to compute the \textbf{Inverse
Document Frequency (IDF)}, which penalizes common words. It is defined
as:

\begin{equation}
    \text{IDF}_i = \log \frac{N}{1 + \text{DF}_i}
\end{equation}

where \(N\) is the total number of text messages and \(\text{DF}_i\) is
the number of messages containing word \(i\). The addition of 1 in the
denominator prevents division by zero.

Once we have the IDF values, we combine them with \textbf{Term Frequency
(TF)}, which measures how often a word appears in a specific message.
The TF-IDF score for word \(i\) in message \(j\) is then computed as:

\begin{equation}
    \text{TFIDF}_{ij} = \log(\text{TF}_{ij} + 1) \cdot \text{IDF}_i
\end{equation}

This formula ensures that frequently used but uninformative words are
downweighted, while rare yet significant words get a higher score. By
transforming our text data this way, we obtain a much more meaningful
numerical representation that improves the classifier's performance!
While in the code cell below I define the vectorization process, I will
not apply it to the data yet since I will be performing a
cross-validation in the model. So, if I applied the TF-IDF now, there
would be data leakage between the training and validation sets.

Additionally, before applying TF-IDF, I also want to remove stopwords,
which are common words like \emph{e} (``and'') or \emph{o} (``the'')
that increase the number of tokens without adding much meaning. While
\texttt{TfidfVectorizer} from \texttt{sklearn} includes a built-in list
of English stopwords, my messages are in Portuguese, so I defined my own
stopword list based on similar principles.

Lastly, I need to lemmatize the text before computing TF-IDF and
removing stopwords. Words with the same meaning often appear in
different forms (\emph{corri}, \emph{correu}, \emph{correndo}), and
lemmatization helps standardize them to a single base form
(\emph{correr}). This reduces the number of unique tokens while
preserving the essential meaning of the text --- just like converting
\emph{running} and \emph{runs} into \emph{run} in English.

\PY{k+kn}{from}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{sklearn}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{feature\PYZus{}extraction}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{text}
\PY{k+kn}{import}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{spacy}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} apply lemmatization}

\PY{n}{nlp} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{spacy}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{load}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{pt\PYZus{}core\PYZus{}news\PYZus{}sm}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}  \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Load Portuguese language model}

\PY{k}{def}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nf}{lemmatize\PYZus{}text}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{text}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{:}
    \PY{n}{doc} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{nlp}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{text}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{n}{lemmatized\PYZus{}words} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{token}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{lemma\PYZus{}} \PY{k}{for} \PY{n}{token} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{n}{doc}\PY{p}{]}  \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Extract lemmas}
    \PY{k}{return} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{ }\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{join}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{lemmatized\PYZus{}words}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n}{train\PYZus{}df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Message}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{train\PYZus{}df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Message}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{apply}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{lemmatize\PYZus{}text}\PY{p}{)}  \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Apply lemmatization to the training set}
\PY{n}{test\PYZus{}df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Message}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{test\PYZus{}df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Message}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{apply}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{lemmatize\PYZus{}text}\PY{p}{)}  \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Apply lemmatization to the test set}

    \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{vai}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{kkkkk}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{kkkkkkkk}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{vou}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{ja}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{q}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{ir}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{pq}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{aqui}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{achar}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{kkkkkkr}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{https}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{tbm}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{kkkkkkkkkkkkk}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Convert stopwords to a list}
\PY{n}{stop\PYZus{}words\PYZus{}pt} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n+nb}{list}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{text}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{ENGLISH\PYZus{}STOP\PYZus{}WORDS}\PY{p}{)} \PY{o}{+} \PY{n}{stopwords}

\PY{n}{vectorizer} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{TfidfVectorizer}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{stop\PYZus{}words}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{stop\PYZus{}words\PYZus{}pt}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{token\PYZus{}pattern}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+s+sa}{r}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZbs{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{b[a\PYZhy{}zA\PYZhy{}Z]+}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZbs{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{b}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \subsection{2.5 Exploratory Data
Analysis}\label{exploratory-data-analysis}

Looking at the summary of our complete chat data (see the table below),
a few interesting things pop out! First, we've exchanged 160,548
messages in total --- pretty impressive. Out of these, I sent 90,980
messages, while Arthur sent 69,568 (which means I talk more\ldots{} not
surprising). When it comes to unique messages, there are 104,097
different ones, so we do repeat ourselves quite a bit. And speaking of
repetition, the most common message in our chat is ``sim'' (``yes''),
which was sent 1,821 times --- looks like we agree with each other a
lot!

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{ }{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{df}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{describe}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

            \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=.5pt, pad at break*=1mm, opacityfill=0]
\prompt{Out}{outcolor}{ }{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
        Sender Message
count   160548  160548
unique       2  106255
top          1     sim
freq     90980    1388
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}
        
    Note that the description above was regarding the whole dataset, before
the preprocessing. Below is the description of the train and test sets.
It is interesting to note that the top messages were ``sim'' (``yes'')
and ``kkkkkkk'' (``hahaha''), which are not very informative in
meanining. This explains the need for the preprocessing described above.

\end{tcolorbox}

\end{tcolorbox}
        
    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{ }{\boxspacing}

       Sender  Message

\end{Verbatim}

    Now, let's analyze the dataset after the TF-IDF transformation. Below,
there is a wordcloud of the most popular words (tokenized) that we use.
The most popular words, like ``baby'' (I know, very cheesy, is how we
call each other), ``ir'' (``go''), ``casa''(``home''), ``bom''
(``good'') and ``dia'' (``morning'') makes sense as they are very common
on our daily conversations. I also made a wordcloud for the test and
training samples. They are very similar to the whole dataset wordcloud,
which shows that the random samples are representative.

As another way to visualize the same information, I plotted a bar plot
of the 20 most common words, and a heatmap of them.

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{ }{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} word cloud for the whole dataset}
\PY{k+kn}{from}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{wordcloud}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{WordCloud}
\PY{k+kn}{import}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{matplotlib}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{pyplot}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k}{as}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{plt}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} apply TF\PYZhy{}IDF}
\PY{n}{tfidf\PYZus{}matrix} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{vectorizer}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{fit\PYZus{}transform}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Message}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Get feature names (words) and their frequencies}
\PY{n}{words} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{vectorizer}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{get\PYZus{}feature\PYZus{}names\PYZus{}out}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{word\PYZus{}frequencies} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{tfidf\PYZus{}matrix}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{sum}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{axis}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{A1}  \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Sum across all documents to get word frequencies}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Create a dictionary of words and their frequencies}
\PY{n}{word\PYZus{}freq\PYZus{}dict} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n+nb}{dict}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n+nb}{zip}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{words}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{word\PYZus{}frequencies}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Create and display the word cloud}
\PY{n}{wordcloud} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{WordCloud}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{width}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{800}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{height}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{400}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{background\PYZus{}color}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{white}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{generate\PYZus{}from\PYZus{}frequencies}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{word\PYZus{}freq\PYZus{}dict}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{figure}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{figsize}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+m+mi}{10}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{8}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{imshow}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{wordcloud}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{interpolation}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{bilinear}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{axis}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{off}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{show}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{figure}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{figsize}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+m+mi}{10}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{8}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Get feature names (words) and their frequencies}
\PY{n}{words\PYZus{}test} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{vectorizer}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{get\PYZus{}feature\PYZus{}names\PYZus{}out}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{word\PYZus{}frequencies\PYZus{}test} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{tfidf\PYZus{}matrix\PYZus{}test}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{sum}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{axis}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{A1}  \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Sum across all documents to get word frequencies}

    \section{4. Model Selection and
Explanation}\label{model-selection-and-explanation}

The Naive Bayes classifier is a simple yet powerful probabilistic
classifier based on Bayes' theorem, with a particular focus on the
multinomial distribution when applied to text data. This distribution is
well-suited for discrete features (such as word counts or TF-IDF values)
and models the probability of a word occurring in a given class.

Bayes' theorem provides a way to update the probability of a hypothesis
based on new evidence. The general form of Bayes' theorem is:

\[
P(C \mid X) = \frac{P(X \mid C) P(C)}{P(X)}
\]

Where:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \$ P(C \mid X) \$ is the \textbf{posterior probability}, or the
  probability that class \(C\) is true given the evidence \(X\).
\item
  \$ P(X \mid C) \$ is the \textbf{likelihood}, or the probability of
  observing the evidence \$ X \$ given the class \$ C \$.
\item
  \$ P(C) \$ is the \textbf{prior probability}, or the probability of
  the class \$ C \$ before seeing any evidence.
\item
  \(P(X)\) is the \textbf{marginal likelihood}, or the probability of
  observing the evidence \$ X \$ across all classes.
\end{itemize}

In text classification, we aim to classify a message \(X\) as belonging
to one of two classes: from me (denoted as class \$ C\_\{\text{me}\} \$)
or from Arthur (denoted as class \$ C\_\{\text{Arthur}\} \$). The
message \$ X \$ is represented as a TF-IDF vector: \$ X = (x\_1, x\_2,
\dots, x\_n) \$, where each \$ x\_i \$ corresponds to the TF-IDF weight
of the \(i\)-th term in our vocabulary.

Using Bayes' theorem, the probability of the message \$ X \$ being from
class \$ C \$ is:

\[
P(C \mid X) = \frac{P(X \mid C) P(C)}{P(X)}
\]

We assume that the words in the message are conditionally independent
given the class. This is the \textbf{naive} assumption in the Naive
Bayes classifier. With this assumption, we can factor the likelihood \$
P(X \mid C) \$ as:

\[
P(X \mid C) = P(x_1, x_2, \dots, x_n \mid C) = \prod_{i=1}^{n} P(x_i \mid C)
\]

Thus, the posterior probability becomes:

\[
P(C \mid X) = \frac{P(C) \prod_{i=1}^{n} P(x_i \mid C)}{P(X)}
\]

In practice, we don't need to calculate \$ P(X) \$ (the marginal
likelihood), since it is the same for all classes. So, we can focus on
maximizing \$ P(C \mid X) \$ with respect to \$ C \$. The class that
maximizes the posterior probability \$ P(C \mid X) \$ is the predicted
class:

\[
\hat{C} = \arg\max_C P(C) \prod_{i=1}^{n} P(x_i \mid C)
\]

To estimate the probabilities \$ P(C) \$ and \$ P(x\_i \mid C) \$, we
use Maximum Likelihood Estimation (MLE). MLE is a method for estimating
the parameters of a statistical model that maximizes the likelihood of
the observed data.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Prior Probability \$ P(C) \$:}
\end{enumerate}

The prior probability of each class is estimated as the proportion of
each class in the training dataset. In this case, as the sampled dataset
is completely balanced, we have that:

\[
P(C_{\text{me}}) = 0.5
\] \[
P(C_{\text{Arthur}}) = 0.5
\]

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{1}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Likelihood \$ P(x\_i \mid C) \$:}
\end{enumerate}

The likelihood \$ P(x\_i \mid C) \$ represents the probability of
observing a word \$ x\_i \$ given a class \$ C \$. Using MLE, we
estimate it as the frequency of word \$ x\_i \$ in class \$ C \$ divided
by the total number of words in that class. Specifically:

\[
P(x_i \mid C) = \frac{\text{Count of } x_i \text{ in class } C}{\text{Total number of words in class } C}
\]

To handle cases where a word might not appear in the training data for a
given class, we use \textbf{Laplace smoothing}. This adjusts the
probabilities by adding a small constant (usually 1) to each count.

\[
P(x_i \mid C) = \frac{\text{Count of } x_i \text{ in class } C + 1}{\text{Total number of words in class } C + V}
\]

Where \$ V \$ is the number of unique words in the vocabulary. Figure 1
illustrates the intuition behind this process.

\textbf{Figure 1.} \emph{Applying Bayes' Theorem to classify a message
as being sent from Arthur or from Mariana.} This figure illustrates how
to calculate the probability of being from a class given the word
``soccer''. On the multinomial naive bayes, this process would be
repeated for all the tokenized words in the message in order to
calculate the likelihood.

The pseudocode below showcases how the Multinomial Naive Bayes will be
applied in the context of this assignment.

    \begin{verbatim}
# Step 1: Preprocess the data
# - Convert the text messages to TF-IDF representations
# - Each message is represented as a vector of TF-IDF values for words/features
# - Prepare training and test datasets (e.g., labeled as 'Mariana - 1' or 'Arthur - 0')

# Step 2: Train the model
# Initialize the prior and likelihood dictionaries for each class (Mariana, Arthur)
for each class in ['Mariana', 'Arthur']:
    # Calculate prior probability P(class) = count(class) / total_samples
    prior[class] = count(class) / total_samples
    
    # Calculate the likelihood P(feature|class) using the multinomial distribution
    for each feature in features:  # features are TF-IDF values for each word
        # Multinomial likelihood: P(feature|class) = (sum(TFIDF(feature in class)) + smoothing) / (sum(TFIDF in class) + V)
        likelihood[class][feature] = (sum(TFIDF of feature in class) + smoothing) / (sum(TFIDF values in class) + V)

# Step 3: Make predictions
for each test_message in test_data:
    posterior = {}
    
    # For each class (Mariana or Arthur), calculate the posterior probability P(class|features)
    for each class in ['Mariana', 'Arthur']:
        # Start with the prior probability P(class)
        posterior[class] = prior[class]
        
        # Multiply by the likelihood of each feature (TF-IDF value) given the class (using multinomial distribution)
        for each feature in test_message:  # test_message is a vector of TF-IDF values
            # P(class|features) = P(class) * P(features|class)
            # Update the probability using the multinomial likelihood
            posterior[class] *= likelihood[class].get(feature, 0)  # handle unseen features by returning 0 if not found
    
    # Choose the class with the highest posterior probability (either 'Mariana' or 'Arthur')
    predicted_class = argmax(posterior)
    
# Step 4: Evaluate model performance (e.g., accuracy, precision)
\end{verbatim}

    \section{5. Training and Validation}\label{training-and-validation}

    Below is my implementation of the multinomial naive bayes classifier
using the sklearn library. I will be using a K-fold cross-validation,
which helps me test how well the model performs on different parts of
the data, ensuring it's not just memorizing specific cases but actually
learning something useful that works across the board.

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{ }{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{k+kn}{import}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{pandas}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k}{as}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{pd}
\PY{k+kn}{import}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{seaborn}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k}{as}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{sns}
\PY{k+kn}{import}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{matplotlib}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{pyplot}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k}{as}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{plt}
\PY{k+kn}{from}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{sklearn}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{model\PYZus{}selection}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{cross\PYZus{}val\PYZus{}score}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{StratifiedKFold}
\PY{k+kn}{from}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{sklearn}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{pipeline}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{Pipeline}
\PY{k+kn}{from}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{sklearn}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{naive\PYZus{}bayes}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{MultinomialNB}
\PY{k+kn}{from}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{sklearn}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{metrics}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{accuracy\PYZus{}score}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{confusion\PYZus{}matrix}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{classification\PYZus{}report}\PY{p}{,}
                             \PY{n}{precision\PYZus{}recall\PYZus{}fscore\PYZus{}support}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{roc\PYZus{}curve}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{auc}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Load training data}
\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{train\PYZus{}df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Message}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}
\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}train} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{train\PYZus{}df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Sender}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Load test data}
\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}test} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{test\PYZus{}df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Message}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}
\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}test} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{test\PYZus{}df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Sender}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Define pipeline: TF\PYZhy{}IDF Vectorizer + Naive Bayes Classifier}
\PY{n}{pipeline\PYZus{}bayes} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{Pipeline}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{[}
    \PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{tfidf}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{vectorizer}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}  \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Fit TF\PYZhy{}IDF on each fold separately}
    \PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{clf}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{MultinomialNB}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}       \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Train Naive Bayes classifier}
\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Perform Stratified K\PYZhy{}Fold Cross\PYZhy{}Validation}
\PY{n}{kf} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{StratifiedKFold}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{n\PYZus{}splits}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{10}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{shuffle}\PY{o}{=}\PY{k+kc}{True}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{random\PYZus{}state}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{42}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{cv\PYZus{}scores} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{cross\PYZus{}val\PYZus{}score}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{pipeline\PYZus{}bayes}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{cv}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{kf}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{scoring}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{accuracy}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Train model on full training set}
\PY{n}{pipeline\PYZus{}bayes}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{fit}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Evaluate on test set}
\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}pred} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{pipeline\PYZus{}bayes}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{predict}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{)}

    Let's dive into my model's performance. So, looking at the confusion
matrix, it seems like the model is doing pretty well at picking up
messages from Arthur, correctly identifying 815 of them as True
Negatives (TN). But there's still some mischief happening with 185
messages that get wrongly labeled as Mariana's (False Positives). On the
flip side, when it comes to my messages, the model nails 533, but misses
467 --- those are the False Negatives (FN), where it mistakenly thinks
you're Arthur.

Now, let's talk numbers: For Arthur's texts, the model has precision at
64\%, meaning when it says ``It's Arthur,'' it's correct about 64\% of
the time. Not bad, but it could get a little sharper. However, it's
really good at identifying Arthur's messages with a recall of 81\%,
meaning it catches 81\% of all messages that actually come from him.

Now, for my messages, the precision is 74\%, meaning when it guesses
``It's Mariana,'' it's right 74\% of the time. Pretty solid! But it
misses about 47\% of my messages, with a recall of 53\%. So, there's
definetely some room for improvement to make sure it doesn't overlook
your texts. The F1-score for my messages is 0.62, which shows a balance
between precision and recall but suggests that it could do better at
both.

As shown by the ROC curve, the model does better than random guesses,
which is great! Overall, it is great at spotting Arthur, but sometimes
it's a little too eager when labeling my messages as his.

    \subsection{6.2 SVM as an alternative
model}\label{svm-as-an-alternative-model}

If I changed the model, would the performance metrics improve?

Let's dive into the magic of Support Vector Machines (SVM) and see how
it can be applied to this classification task. Given that the messages
have already been preprocessed using TF-IDF, we can think of each
message as a vector of features that captures the importance of
different words in the text.

In essence, an SVM is a supervised learning algorithm that constructs a
hyperplane (or a set of hyperplanes in higher dimensions) that best
separates data into different classes. The goal of the SVM is to
maximize the \textbf{margin} between the two classes while correctly
classifying as many data points as possible. The \textbf{margin} is
defined as the distance between the hyperplane and the closest data
points, which are known as \textbf{support vectors}. These support
vectors are critical since they are the points that define the decision
boundary.

Mathematically, the optimization problem SVM tries to solve can be
formulated as:

\[
\underset{\mathbf{w}, b}{\text{maximize}} \ \frac{2}{\|\mathbf{w}\|}
\]

where (\$\mathbf{w}
\(\) is the vector normal to the hyperplane, and (\)b\$) is the bias
term. This equation essentially tells us that we want to maximize the
distance between the hyperplane and the support vectors to ensure a
robust classifier.

However, the real world isn't always as neat as we'd like it to be, so
not all data points can be perfectly separated by a hyperplane. To
account for this, we introduce \textbf{slack variables} (\(\xi_i\)) for
each data point, which allow for some misclassification if necessary.
This leads to the following optimization problem:

\[
\underset{\mathbf{w}, b, \xi_i}{\text{minimize}} \ \frac{1}{2} \|\mathbf{w}\|^2 + C \sum_{i=1}^{n} \xi_i
\]

Here, (\(C\)) is a regularization parameter that controls the trade-off
between maximizing the margin and minimizing classification errors. A
larger (\(C\)) reduces the allowable misclassification but may lead to
overfitting, whereas a smaller (\(C\)) allows for more
misclassification, favoring a simpler, more generalized model. The term
(\$\frac{1}{2} \textbar{}\mathbf{w}\textbar\^{}2 \$) ensures that we
minimize the complexity of the model, helping it generalize better to
unseen data.

Once the model has been trained and the hyperplane has been determined,
we can use it to classify new messages. Given a new message, we convert
it into a TF-IDF vector and compute its position relative to the
hyperplane. If the new message lies on the ``Arthur'' side of the
hyperplane, it is classified as a message from him, and if it falls on
the ``Mariana'' side, it's classified as one of mine. Figure 2 is an
illustration of how SVM works.

\begin{figure}
\centering
\caption{Geometric-description-of-SVM-for-a-binary-classification-problem.png}
\end{figure}

\textbf{Figure 2.} \emph{Geometric description of SVM.} Source:
https://www.google.com/url?sa=i\&url=https\%3A\%2F\%2Fwww.researchgate.net\%2Ffigure\%2FGeometric-description-of-SVM-for-a-binary-classification-problem\_fig14\_224050262\&psig=AOvVaw2LZMvwwj0sQ4pvY2qpBHyw\&ust=1740355276565000\&source=images\&cd=vfe\&opi=89978449\&ved=0CBcQjhxqFwoTCLjwnuW-2IsDFQAAAAAdAAAAABAh

Below is a pseudocode that summarizes the implementation of SVM:

\begin{verbatim}
# Step 1: Preprocess the data
# - Convert the text messages to TF-IDF representations
# - Each message is represented as a vector of TF-IDF values for words/features
# - Prepare training and test datasets (e.g., labeled as 'Mariana - 1' or 'Arthur - 0')

# Step 2: Initialize the SVM model
# Choose a kernel function (e.g., linear, RBF, or polynomial)
# Set the regularization parameter C
# Initialize weight vector w and bias b

# Step 3: Train the SVM model
for each data_point (x_i, y_i) in training_data:
    # If the data point is on the wrong side of the margin:
    if y_i * (w * x_i + b) < 1:
        # Update the weight vector w and bias b using the gradient of the hinge loss
        w = w - learning_rate * (w - C * y_i * x_i)
        b = b - learning_rate * (-C * y_i)
    else:
        # If the data point is on the correct side, no update needed
        pass

# Step 4: Make predictions on the test set
for each test_message x_test in test_data:
    # Calculate the decision function f(x_test) = w * x_test + b
    f_x_test = w * x_test + b

    # Classify as 'Mariana' if f(x_test) > 0, else classify as 'Arthur'
    if f_x_test > 0:
        predicted_class = 'Mariana'
    else:
        predicted_class = 'Arthur'

# Step 5: Evaluate model performance (e.g., accuracy, precision, recall)
\end{verbatim}

    The beauty of SVM lies in its ability to handle high-dimensional data
efficiently, especially when combined with TF-IDF for text
classification. By using the inner product
(\(\langle \mathbf{x}_i, \mathbf{x}_j \rangle\)), we can even map our
data to a higher-dimensional space using the \textbf{kernel trick},
which allows us to separate non-linearly separable data by transforming
it into a space where a hyperplane can be used effectively.

In conclusion, SVM is an ideal choice for this classification problem
due to its ability to maximize the margin, handle high-dimensional
spaces efficiently, and deal with noisy data through the use of slack
variables. It provides a clear decision boundary that can separate the
messages with precision, ensuring that the model doesn't simply memorize
specific examples but generalizes well to new, unseen messages.

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Load training data}
\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{train\PYZus{}df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Message}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}
\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}train} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{train\PYZus{}df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Sender}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Load test data}
\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}test} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{test\PYZus{}df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Message}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}
\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}test} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{test\PYZus{}df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Sender}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Define pipeline: TF\PYZhy{}IDF Vectorizer + SVM Classifier}
\PY{n}{pipeline} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{Pipeline}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{[}
    \PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{tfidf}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{vectorizer}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}  \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Use TfidfVectorizer defined earlier}
    \PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{clf}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{SVC}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{probability}\PY{o}{=}\PY{k+kc}{True}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}  \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Use SVM classifier and enable probability estimates}
\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Perform Stratified K\PYZhy{}Fold Cross\PYZhy{}Validation}
\PY{n}{kf} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{StratifiedKFold}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{n\PYZus{}splits}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{10}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{shuffle}\PY{o}{=}\PY{k+kc}{True}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{random\PYZus{}state}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{42}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{cv\PYZus{}scores} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{cross\PYZus{}val\PYZus{}score}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{pipeline}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{cv}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{kf}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{scoring}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{accuracy}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Print cross\PYZhy{}validation results}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Mean Accuracy (Cross\PYZhy{}Validation): }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{cv\PYZus{}scores}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{mean}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{l+s+si}{:}\PY{l+s+s2}{.4f}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Standard Deviation: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{cv\PYZus{}scores}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{std}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{l+s+si}{:}\PY{l+s+s2}{.4f}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Train model on full training set}
\PY{n}{pipeline}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{fit}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Evaluate on test set}
\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}pred} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{pipeline}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{predict}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Compute accuracy and classification report}
\PY{n}{test\PYZus{}accuracy} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{accuracy\PYZus{}score}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}pred}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{report} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{classification\PYZus{}report}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}pred}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{target\PYZus{}names}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Arthur}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Mariana}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Test Accuracy: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{test\PYZus{}accuracy}\PY{l+s+si}{:}\PY{l+s+s2}{.4f}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Classification Report:}\PY{l+s+se}{\PYZbs{}n}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{report}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Compute precision, recall, and F1\PYZhy{}score}
\PY{n}{precision}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{recall}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{f1}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{\PYZus{}} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{precision\PYZus{}recall\PYZus{}fscore\PYZus{}support}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}pred}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{average}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{weighted}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n+nb}{print}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Precision: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{precision}\PY{l+s+si}{:}\PY{l+s+s2}{.4f}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{, Recall: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{recall}\PY{l+s+si}{:}\PY{l+s+s2}{.4f}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{, F1\PYZhy{}score: }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{f1}\PY{l+s+si}{:}\PY{l+s+s2}{.4f}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Confusion Matrix}
\PY{n}{conf\PYZus{}matrix} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{confusion\PYZus{}matrix}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}pred}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Plot Confusion Matrix}

\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{xlabel}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Predicted}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{show}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} ROC Curve}

\PY{n}{fpr}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{tpr}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{\PYZus{}} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{roc\PYZus{}curve}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}test}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{astype}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n+nb}{int}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}prob}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{roc\PYZus{}auc} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{auc}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{fpr}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{tpr}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{xlim}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+m+mf}{0.0}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mf}{1.0}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{xlabel}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{False Positive Rate}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{ylabel}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{True Positive Rate}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{title}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Receiver Operating Characteristic (ROC) Curve}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{legend}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{loc}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{lower right}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}

    Even though the expectations for SVM were high, the results are a bit
disappointing. As shown by the plot and the metrics table, the
performance was quite similar to the one from the multinomial naive
bayes.

    \section{7. Visualization and
Insights}\label{visualization-and-insights}

    To figure out how my naive bayes model is doing, I took a look at the
log probabilities for the top 30 words in each class. When I compared
the words with the highest P(X\textbar Mariana) and P(X\textbar Arthur),
I noticed there's a bit of overlap. This could be the ``noise'' messing
with the model's ability to tell us apart. Honestly, I think Arthur and
I have been together for so long that we have absorbed each other's
vocabularies, which is making it tricky for the model to distinguish
between us based on just the words we use. The fact that a more robust
model such as SVM could not improve much the results shows that the
problem with the performance is not blamed on the model choice.

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Sort tokens by probability (descending and ascending)}
\PY{n}{sorted\PYZus{}indices\PYZus{}desc} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{np}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{argsort}\PY{p}{(}\PY{o}{\PYZhy{}}\PY{n}{feature\PYZus{}prob\PYZus{}arthur}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{[}\PY{p}{:}\PY{l+m+mi}{30}\PY{p}{]}  \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Top 30}
\PY{n}{sorted\PYZus{}indices\PYZus{}asc} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{np}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{argsort}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{feature\PYZus{}prob\PYZus{}arthur}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{[}\PY{p}{:}\PY{l+m+mi}{30}\PY{p}{]}    \PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Bottom 30}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Prepare data for plotting}
\PY{n}{top\PYZus{}tokens} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{tokens}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{sorted\PYZus{}indices\PYZus{}desc}\PY{p}{]}
\PY{n}{top\PYZus{}probs} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{feature\PYZus{}prob\PYZus{}arthur}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{sorted\PYZus{}indices\PYZus{}desc}\PY{p}{]}
\PY{n}{low\PYZus{}tokens} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{tokens}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{sorted\PYZus{}indices\PYZus{}asc}\PY{p}{]}
\PY{n}{low\PYZus{}probs} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{feature\PYZus{}prob\PYZus{}arthur}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{sorted\PYZus{}indices\PYZus{}asc}\PY{p}{]}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Plot top 30 tokens}
\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{figure}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{figsize}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+m+mi}{15}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{6}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{bar}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{top\PYZus{}tokens}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{top\PYZus{}probs}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{color}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{blue}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{alpha}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mf}{0.7}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{xticks}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{rotation}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{90}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{title}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Top 30 Tokens with Highest P(X|Arthur)}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{ylabel}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{P(X|Arthur)}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{xlabel}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Tokens}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{tight\PYZus{}layout}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{show}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Plot bottom 30 tokens}
\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{figure}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{figsize}\PY{o}{=}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+m+mi}{15}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+m+mi}{6}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{bar}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{low\PYZus{}tokens}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{low\PYZus{}probs}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{color}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{red}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{alpha}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mf}{0.7}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{xticks}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{rotation}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{90}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{title}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Bottom 30 Tokens with Lowest P(X|Arthur)}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{ylabel}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{P(X|Arthur)}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{plt}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{xlabel}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{Tokens}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{)}

    \section{8. Transfer Learning: leveraging BERT
model}\label{transfer-learning-leveraging-bert-model}

\subsection{8.1 Using BERT as a
Classifier}\label{using-bert-as-a-classifier}

It's time to level up the text classification. Instead of treating words
as isolated units, BERT (Bidirectional Encoder Representations from
Transformers) reads the whole sentence at once, both left to right and
right to left. This means it understands that ``I love you'' and ``You
love I'' aren't quite the same vibe. It is a deep learning model
designed for natural language processing (NLP) tasks, and its network
structure can be observed in Figure 3.

BERT is like that one friend who remembers everything you say, catches
all the hidden meanings, and still knows what you meant even if you
phrased it weirdly. Instead of just looking at words, it learns context,
nuance, and style, which is exactly what we need to distinguish between
me and my boyfriend's messages.

So let's bring in PyTorch, hook it up with a pre-trained Multi-language
BERT, and let the magic happen.

\subsubsection{8.1.1 BERT Tokenizer}\label{bert-tokenizer}

To kick things off, I'm using my pre-processed balanced dataset and
splitting it into training, validation, and test sets, making sure the
model gets a fair mix of learning, fine-tuning, and evaluation. Next,
I'm loading a BERT tokenizer to convert text messages into a format the
model can understand. Neural networks cannot process raw text directly,
so the text must be tokenized using the WordPiece tokenizer. Each word
(or subword, thanks to WordPiece tokenization) is mapped to a
high-dimensional vector, such that each input sequence is converted into
a set of embeddings:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Token Embeddings: Representing the actual words or subwords. In Figure
  3, for example, we can see the sentence ``Dynamic changes of soil
  erosion in Wuqi County'', in which each token gets its embedding (in
  the Token Embedding row), including special tokens like {[}CLS{]} and
  {[}SEP{]}.
\item
  Segment Embeddings: Indicating whether a token belongs to sentence A
  or sentence B (useful for tasks like sentence-pair classification). In
  figure 3, the first few words belong to segment A, while the later
  ones belong to segment B.
\item
  Positional Encodings: Since transformers do not have a built-in sense
  of order, positional encodings are added to retain word order
  information. In figure 3, we can see that the encoding is following
  the order of the sentence.
\end{itemize}

Given an input sequence \(X = (x_1, x_2, ..., x_n)\), the embedding
vector for each token is computed as \(E_i = T_i + S_i + P_i\), where:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \(T_i\) is the token embedding,
\item
  \(S_i\) is the segment embedding,
\item
  \(P_i\) is the positional encoding.
\end{itemize}

This final embedding representation is what gets passed into the BERT
Transformer layers.

In the code cell below, to keep things structured, I'm wrapping
everything into a custom PyTorch \texttt{Dataset} class, where each
message is tokenized and paired with its sender label. Finally, I'm
setting up \texttt{DataLoaders} with batching and shuffling (so the
model doesn't get too comfortable with patterns), ensuring efficient
training and evaluation.

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{11}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} import new libraries}
\PY{k+kn}{import}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{torch}
\PY{k+kn}{import}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{os}
\PY{k+kn}{from}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{torch}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{utils}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{data}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{Dataset}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{DataLoader}
\PY{k+kn}{from}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{transformers}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{AutoTokenizer}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{AutoModelForSequenceClassification}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{AdamW}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{get\PYZus{}scheduler}
\PY{k+kn}{from}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{sklearn}\PY{n+nn}{.}\PY{n+nn}{model\PYZus{}selection}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{train\PYZus{}test\PYZus{}split}
\PY{k+kn}{import}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{evaluate}
\PY{k+kn}{from}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{tqdm}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k+kn}{import} \PY{n}{tqdm}
\PY{k+kn}{import}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{numpy}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{k}{as}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nn}{np}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    Note that to make sure that my model runs faster, I will sample 20k
messages from the complete dataset, and I will make sure the classes are
balanced.

\prompt{In}{incolor}{35}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Step 1: Combine full dataset (if needed)}
\PY{n}{full\PYZus{}df} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{pd}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{concat}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{train\PYZus{}0}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{train\PYZus{}1}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{test\PYZus{}0}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{test\PYZus{}1}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{reset\PYZus{}index}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{drop}\PY{o}{=}\PY{k+kc}{True}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Step 2: Sample training data (9k from each class)}
\PY{n}{train\PYZus{}0\PYZus{}sample} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{train\PYZus{}0}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{sample}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{n}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{9000}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{random\PYZus{}state}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{42}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{train\PYZus{}1\PYZus{}sample} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{train\PYZus{}1}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{sample}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{n}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{9000}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{random\PYZus{}state}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{42}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{train\PYZus{}df\PYZus{}bert} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{pd}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{concat}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{train\PYZus{}0\PYZus{}sample}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{train\PYZus{}1\PYZus{}sample}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{sample}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{frac}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{random\PYZus{}state}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{42}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Remove training samples from original data to avoid overlap}
\PY{n}{remaining\PYZus{}0} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{train\PYZus{}0}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{drop}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{train\PYZus{}0\PYZus{}sample}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{index}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{remaining\PYZus{}1} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{train\PYZus{}1}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{drop}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{train\PYZus{}1\PYZus{}sample}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{index}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Step 3: Sample validation data (1k from each class)}
\PY{n}{val\PYZus{}0\PYZus{}sample} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{remaining\PYZus{}0}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{sample}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{n}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{1000}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{random\PYZus{}state}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{43}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{val\PYZus{}1\PYZus{}sample} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{remaining\PYZus{}1}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{sample}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{n}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{1000}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{random\PYZus{}state}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{43}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{val\PYZus{}df\PYZus{}bert} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{pd}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{concat}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{val\PYZus{}0\PYZus{}sample}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{val\PYZus{}1\PYZus{}sample}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{sample}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{frac}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{random\PYZus{}state}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{43}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Step 4: Sample testing data (1k from each class)}
\PY{n}{test\PYZus{}0\PYZus{}sample} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{test\PYZus{}0}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{sample}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{n}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{1000}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{random\PYZus{}state}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{44}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n}{test\PYZus{}df\PYZus{}bert} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{pd}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{concat}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{test\PYZus{}0\PYZus{}sample}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{test\PYZus{}1\PYZus{}sample}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{sample}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{frac}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{random\PYZus{}state}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{44}\PY{p}{)}

    Now let's apply the tokenization.

    \begin{tcolorbox}[breakable, size=fbox, boxrule=1pt, pad at break*=1mm,colback=cellbackground, colframe=cellborder]
\prompt{In}{incolor}{51}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Split into train, validation, and test sets}
\PY{n}{val\PYZus{}df} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{val\PYZus{}df\PYZus{}bert} 
\PY{n}{train\PYZus{}df} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{train\PYZus{}df\PYZus{}bert}
\PY{n}{test\PYZus{}df} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{test\PYZus{}df\PYZus{}bert} 

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Load tokenizer}
\PY{n}{model\PYZus{}name} \PY{o}{=} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{bert\PYZhy{}base\PYZhy{}multilingual\PYZhy{}cased}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}
\PY{n}{tokenizer} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{AutoTokenizer}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{from\PYZus{}pretrained}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{model\PYZus{}name}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Custom PyTorch Dataset}
\PY{k}{class}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nc}{MessageDataset}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{Dataset}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{:}
    \PY{k}{def}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nf+fm}{\PYZus{}\PYZus{}init\PYZus{}\PYZus{}}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n+nb+bp}{self}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{tokenizer}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{:}
        \PY{n+nb+bp}{self}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{messages} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Message}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{tolist}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
        \PY{n+nb+bp}{self}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{labels} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{df}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Sender}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{astype}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n+nb}{int}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{tolist}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
        \PY{n+nb+bp}{self}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{tokenizer} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{tokenizer}

    \PY{k}{def}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nf+fm}{\PYZus{}\PYZus{}len\PYZus{}\PYZus{}}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n+nb+bp}{self}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{:}
        \PY{k}{return} \PY{n+nb}{len}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n+nb+bp}{self}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{messages}\PY{p}{)}

    \PY{k}{def}\PY{+w}{ }\PY{n+nf+fm}{\PYZus{}\PYZus{}getitem\PYZus{}\PYZus{}}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n+nb+bp}{self}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{idx}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{:}
        \PY{n}{encoding} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n+nb+bp}{self}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{tokenizer}\PY{p}{(}
            \PY{n+nb+bp}{self}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{messages}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{idx}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{,} 
            \PY{n}{padding}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{max\PYZus{}length}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} 
            \PY{n}{truncation}\PY{o}{=}\PY{k+kc}{True}\PY{p}{,} 
            \PY{n}{max\PYZus{}length}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{75}\PY{p}{,}  
            \PY{n}{return\PYZus{}tensors}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{pt}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}
        \PY{p}{)}
        \PY{k}{return} \PY{p}{\PYZob{}}
            \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{input\PYZus{}ids}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n}{encoding}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{input\PYZus{}ids}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{squeeze}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}  
            \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{attention\PYZus{}mask}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n}{encoding}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{attention\PYZus{}mask}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{squeeze}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}  
            \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{label}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n}{torch}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{tensor}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n+nb+bp}{self}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{labels}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{idx}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{dtype}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{torch}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{long}\PY{p}{)}
        \PY{p}{\PYZcb{}}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Create datasets}
\PY{n}{train\PYZus{}dataset} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{MessageDataset}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{train\PYZus{}df}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{tokenizer}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{val\PYZus{}dataset} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{MessageDataset}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{val\PYZus{}df}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{tokenizer}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{test\PYZus{}dataset} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{MessageDataset}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{test\PYZus{}df}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{tokenizer}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Create DataLoaders}
\PY{n}{batch\PYZus{}size} \PY{o}{=} \PY{l+m+mi}{32}
\PY{n}{train\PYZus{}loader} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{DataLoader}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{train\PYZus{}dataset}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{batch\PYZus{}size}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{batch\PYZus{}size}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{shuffle}\PY{o}{=}\PY{k+kc}{True}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{num\PYZus{}workers}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{val\PYZus{}loader} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{DataLoader}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{val\PYZus{}dataset}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{batch\PYZus{}size}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{batch\PYZus{}size}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{shuffle}\PY{o}{=}\PY{k+kc}{False}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{test\PYZus{}loader} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{DataLoader}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{test\PYZus{}dataset}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{batch\PYZus{}size}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{batch\PYZus{}size}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{shuffle}\PY{o}{=}\PY{k+kc}{False}\PY{p}{)}
\end{Verbatim}
\end{tcolorbox}

    \subsubsection{8.1.2 BERT Pre-Training}\label{bert-pre-training}

Now that the input is prepared for BERT, I will load the pre-trained
model. Here is where the transfer learning comes in! Before jumping into
the code, I will explain how BERT is pre-trained.

BERT is pretrained using two innovative tasks: \textbf{Masked Language
Modeling (MLM)} and \textbf{Next Sentence Prediction (NSP)}. These two
objectives help BERT develop a deep understanding of language context
and relationships. Let's break them down!

The masked language modeling (MLM) objective is designed to teach the
model how to predict missing words based on their surrounding context.
To accomplish this, BERT first tokenizes the input text and replaces a
subset of tokens with a special \texttt{{[}MASK{]}} token. Specifically,
15\% of the tokens are randomly selected, and among these, 80\% are
replaced with \texttt{{[}MASK{]}}, 10\% are replaced with a random word,
and 10\% remain unchanged. This ensures that the model does not overfit
to simply recognizing \texttt{{[}MASK{]}} tokens but instead learns
robust contextual representations.

Once the masked tokens are introduced, the entire sequence is passed
through the Transformer layers of BERT. Each token in the sequence is
transformed into a contextualized vector representation by attending to
all other tokens in both left and right directions. The output of the
final Transformer layer contains hidden state representations that
encapsulate contextual information for each token, including the masked
ones. To predict a masked token, BERT uses the hidden representation
corresponding to the masked position as input to a feedforward neural
network (FFNN). This FFNN consists of a linear transformation followed
by a softmax function that maps the hidden state to a probability
distribution over the entire vocabulary.

Mathematically, if we denote the masked input sequence as \(\tilde{X}\)
and let \(h_i\) be the hidden representation of the masked token at
position \(i\), the probability of predicting token \(x_i\) is computed
as:

\[
P(x_i | \tilde{X}) = \text{softmax}(W h_i + b)
\]

where \(W\) is a weight matrix that maps the hidden state to logits over
the vocabulary, and \(b\) is a bias term. The softmax function ensures
that the model produces a probability distribution across all possible
tokens. The objective during training is to maximize the likelihood of
predicting the correct token at each masked position, which is achieved
by minimizing the cross-entropy loss:

\[
\mathcal{L}_{MLM} = -\sum_{v \in V} y_v \log P(x_i = v | \tilde{X})
\]

where \(V\) is the vocabulary size, and \(y_v\) is a one-hot encoded
vector representing the correct token. By minimizing this loss, BERT
effectively learns to maximize the probability of the correct token
given its context. This process forces BERT to deeply understand word
relationships and semantic structures, leading to strong contextual
embeddings that can be used for a variety of downstream natural language
processing tasks. Figure 4 illustrates this process.

Now, to help BERT understand long-range dependencies and sentence-level
context (useful for tasks like question-answering), it is trained to
determine whether \textbf{one sentence logically follows another}. This
is the \textbf{Next Sentence Prediction (NSP)} part.

In addition to masked language modeling, BERT is also pre-trained with
the next sentence prediction (NSP) objective, which helps it understand
relationships between sentences. This is particularly useful for tasks
such as question-answering and natural language inference, where
determining whether one sentence logically follows another is crucial.
During pre-training, BERT receives pairs of sentences as input. Half of
the time, the second sentence is the actual next sentence in the
original text (labeled as ``IsNext''), and the other half of the time,
it is a randomly sampled sentence from the corpus (labeled as
``NotNext''). This binary classification task trains BERT to distinguish
between coherent and unrelated sentence pairs.

To implement NSP, BERT processes both sentences together as a single
input sequence. The input is formatted by concatenating the two
sentences with a special \texttt{{[}SEP{]}} token separating them. A
\texttt{{[}CLS{]}} token is added at the beginning, serving as a special
classification token. Each token in the sequence is embedded using token
embeddings, segment embeddings, and positional embeddings, which was
already explained above. The resulting tokenized sequence is then passed
through multiple Transformer layers, where self-attention mechanisms
allow BERT to model relationships between all tokens in both sentences.

After processing the sequence, the final hidden state representation of
the \texttt{{[}CLS{]}} token, denoted as \$ h\_\{\text{CLS}\}\$, is used
for the next sentence prediction task. Similarly to MLM, this
representation is passed through a feedforward neural network (FFNN)
followed by a softmax function to produce a probability distribution
over the two possible labels:

\[
P(y | A, B) = \text{softmax}(W h_{\text{CLS}} + b)
\]

where \(W\) is a learned weight matrix, and \(b\) is a bias term. The
model is trained by minimizing the cross-entropy loss:

\[
\mathcal{L}_{NSP} = -\sum_{y \in \{\text{IsNext}, \text{NotNext}\}} y \log P(y | A, B)
\]

where \(y\) is the true label indicating whether the second sentence
follows the first. By optimizing this loss function, BERT learns to
capture long-range dependencies and semantic coherence between
sentences. This ability makes it highly effective for tasks that require
understanding how different sentences relate to each other, such as text
summarization, dialogue systems, and multi-turn question answering.

Thankfully all this work has already been done for me, so now I just
need to load the pre-trained model in the code cell below.
